Global Well-Posedness of Contact Lines: 2D Navier-Stokes Flow Thanks: Y. Guo was supported by NSF Grant DMS-2106650. Thanks: I. Tice was supported by NSF Grant DMS-2508400. Thanks: L. Wu was supported by NSF Grant DMS-2104775 and DMS-2405161. Thanks: X. Yang was supported by NSF Grant DMS-2405051. Thanks: Y. Zheng was supported by NSFC Grant 11901350 and 12371172.
Yan Guo, Ian Tice, Lei Wu, Xiaoding Yang, Yunrui Zheng

Abstract
. Based on the global a priori estimates in [Guo-Tice, J. Eur. Math. Soc. (2024)], we establish the well-posedness of a viscous fluid model satisfying the dynamic law for the contact line 𝒲 ⁡ ( ∂ t ζ ⁡ ( ± ℓ , t ) ) = [ [ γ ] ] ∓ σ ​ ∂ 1 ζ ( 1 + | ∂ 1 ζ | 2 ) 1 / 2 ​ ( ± ℓ , t ) \mathscr{W}(\partial_{t}\zeta(\pm\ell,t))=[\![\gamma]\!]\mp\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}(\pm\ell,t) in a 2D domain, where ζ ⁡ ( x 1 , t ) \zeta(x_{1},t) is a free surface with two contact points ζ ⁡ ( ± ℓ , t ) \zeta(\pm\ell,t) , [ [ γ ] ] [\![\gamma]\!] and σ \sigma are constants characterizing the solid-fluid-gas free energy, and 𝒲 \mathscr{W} is the contact point velocity response function. Our approach, which is motivated by the natural energy-dissipation structure of the problem, relies on constructing a pressureless weak solution to the linearized problem for the coupled velocity and free interface, via a Galerkin approximation with a time-dependent basis and an artificial regularization for the capillary operator.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q30, 35R35, 76D45; Secondary 35B40, 76E17, 76E99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Problem Formulation

We consider a
2
D open top vessel as a bounded, connected open set
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
which consists of two disjoint sections as well as their intersection, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{top}\cup\mathcal{V}_{bot}\cup I
.
We refer to Figure 1 for an example.

\mathcal{V}_{top}
\mathcal{V}_{bot}
I

Figure 1.
A vessel
\mathcal{V}
.

\Omega(t)
\Sigma(t)
\Sigma_{s}(t)

Figure 2.
The domain
\Omega(t)
.

Assume that the “top” part
\mathcal{V}_{top}
is a rectangular channel

\mathcal{V}_{top}:=\mathcal{V}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:-\ell<y_{1}<\ell,0\leq y_{2}<L\}

for some
\ell
,
L>0
, where
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is the upper half plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y_{2}\geq 0\}
. Similarly, we write the “bottom” part as

\mathcal{V}_{bot}:=\mathcal{V}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}=\mathcal{V}\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y_{2}\leq 0\}.

Clearly,
I=(-\ell,\ell)\times\{0\}
. In addition, we also assume that the boundary
\partial\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{V}
is
C^{2}
away from the points
(\pm\ell,L)
.

We consider a viscous incompressible fluid filling
\mathcal{V}_{bot}
entirely and
\mathcal{V}_{top}
partially. More precisely, we assume that the fluid occupies the domain
\Omega(t)
with a top free surface,

\Omega(t):=\mathcal{V}_{bot}\cup\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:-\ell<y_{1}<\ell,\ 0<y_{2}<\zeta(y_{1},t)\big\},

where the free surface
\zeta(y_{1},t)
is assumed to be a graph of the function
\zeta:[-\ell,\ell]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
0<\zeta(\pm\ell,t)\leq L
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which means the fluid does not spill out of the top domain. For simplicity, we write the free surface as
\Sigma(t)=\{y_{2}=\zeta(y_{1},t)\}
and the interface between fluid and solid as
\Sigma_{s}(t)=\partial\Omega(t)\backslash\Sigma(t)
.
We refer to Figure 2 for an illustration of domain.

For each
t>0
, the fluid is described by its velocity and pressure
(u,P):\Omega(t)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
which is governed by the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u+u\cdot\nabla u+\nabla P-\mu\Delta u=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega(t),\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega(t),\\
&S(P,u)\nu=g\zeta\nu-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta)\nu\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma(t),\\
&\big(S(P,u)\nu-\beta u\big)\cdot\tau=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}(t),\\
&u\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}(t),\\
&\partial_{t}\zeta=u\cdot\nu=u_{2}-u_{1}\partial_{1}\zeta\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma(t),\\
\rule{0.0pt}{-15.00002pt}&\partial_{t}\zeta(\pm\ell,t)=\mathscr{V}\left([\![\gamma]\!]\mp\sigma\tfrac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}(\pm\ell,t)\right).\end{aligned}\right.

In the above system (
1.1
),
S(P,u)
is the viscous stress tensor

S(P,u):=PI-\mu\mathbb{D}u
,
where
I
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix,
\mu>0
is the coefficient of viscosity,
\mathbb{D}u=\nabla u+\nabla^{T}u
is the symmetric gradient of
u
for
\nabla^{T}u
the transpose of the matrix
\nabla u
. The pressure unknown
P=\bar{P}+gy_{2}-P_{atm}
is obtained by adjusting the actual pressure
\bar{P}
by the constant atmospheric pressure
P_{atm}
and the background hydrostatic pressure
gy_{2}
. The vector
\nu
is the outward unit normal,
\tau
is the unit tangent,
\sigma>0
is the coefficient of surface tension,

\mathcal{H}(\zeta)=\partial_{1}\left(\tfrac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}\right)

is twice the mean curvature of the free surface, and
\beta>0
is the Navier-slip friction coefficient on the vessel walls. The boundary conditions on
\Sigma_{s}(t)
are called Navier-slip conditions.
\mathscr{V}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the contact point velocity response function which is a
C^{2}
increasing diffeomorphism satisfying
\mathscr{V}(0)=0
.
[\![\gamma]\!]:=\gamma_{sv}-\gamma_{sf}
for
\gamma_{sv}
,
\gamma_{sf}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\gamma_{sv}
,
\gamma_{sf}
are measures of the free-energy per unit length with respect to the solid-vapor and solid-fluid intersection. We also assume that the Young relation

|[\![\gamma]\!]|<\sigma
(
[
26
]
),
which is necessary for the equilibrium state we will consider in the following. For convenience, we introduce the inverse function
\mathscr{W}
of
\mathscr{V}
and rewrite the final equation in (
1.1
) as

(1.2)

\mathscr{W}\big(\partial_{t}\zeta(\pm\ell,t)\big)=[\![\gamma]\!]\mp\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}(\pm\ell,t).

Contact points in 2D and contact lines in 3D domain have been studied for a long time, and we may refer to
[
4
, de Gennes]
for an overview. There is an extensive literature dealing with the free boundary problems of contact points or contact lines, and so we will only mention some works closely related to our background settings.

The analysis for Navier-slip conditions with contact angle can be found in
[
15
, Ren-E]
,
[
3
, Cox]
and
[
10
, Guo-Tice]
, thee latter of which we refer to for a lengthier overview. Note that the model (
1.1
) allows both the contact points and contact angles to move over time.
The global a priori estimates for (
1.1
) are given by
[
10
, Guo-Tice]
. When the contact angle is fixed at
\frac{\pi}{2}
, the well-posedness of 2D contact points problem in the Navier-Stokes flow is studied by
[
17
, Schweizer]
and the 3D contact line problem with an additional periodic direction is studied by
[
1
, Bodea]
.

There are also many results for the stationary problem of contact points or lines. The solvability of stationary Navier-Stokes problem for dynamic contact angles and moving contact lines are studied by
[
12
, Kröner]
and
[
18
, Socolosky]
, respectively. The local well-posedness and stability for the 2D moving contact points in the Stokes flow are studied by
[
9
, Guo-Tice]
and
[
23
, Tice-Zheng]
. The existence results for the Navier-Stokes equations for both static and dynamic contact points and lines in weighted Hölder spaces are proved by
[
19
,
20
, Solonnikov]
. The 3D contact line problem for the stationary Navier-Stokes equations with fixed angle
\frac{\pi}{2}
is studied by
[
11
, Jin]
.

The contact angles are also closely related to the droplet problem. The stability problem for droplets governed by the Stokes equations is studied by
[
22
, Tice-Wu]
. The well-posedness for droplets governed by Darcy’s law is studied by
[
13
, Knüpfer-Masmoudi]
.

Despite
[
10
, Guo-Tice]
, there is no construction of solutions with dynamical contact line law (
1.2
), which
must be
compatible with all the other boundary conditions. The purpose of this paper is to establish the first result for the global well-posedness of the 2D Navier-Stokes contact line problem. It is known that (
1.1
) admits an equilibrium state with
u=0
,
P=P_{0}=\text{constant}
and
\zeta(y_{1},t)=\zeta_{0}(y_{1})
satisfying

(1.3)

P_{0}=g\zeta_{0}-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta_{0})\quad\text{on}\ (-\ell,\ell),\quad\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2}}}(\pm\ell)=\pm[\![\gamma]\!].

For the well-posedness of (
1.3
) we refer to
[
9
, Guo-Tice]
or
[
6
, Finn]
.

This equilibrium allows us to transform the free surface problem into a fixed domain. Let
\zeta_{0}\in C^{\infty}[-\ell,\ell]
be the equilibrium surface given by (
1.3
). We then define the equilibrium domain

\Omega:=\mathcal{V}_{b}\cup\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|-\ell<x_{1}<\ell,0<x_{2}<\zeta_{0}(x_{1})\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The boundary
\partial\Omega
of the equilibrium
\Omega
is defined by

\partial\Omega:=\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{s}
,
where

\Sigma:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|-\ell<x_{1}<\ell,x_{2}=\zeta_{0}(x_{1})\}
and
\Sigma_{s}=\partial\Omega\backslash\Sigma
.
Here
\Sigma
is the equilibrium free surface. Denote the corner angle
\omega\in(0,\pi)
of
\Omega
formed by the fluid and solid.
We assume that the function
\zeta(y_{1},t)
of free surface is the perturbation of
\zeta_{0}(y_{1})
as

\zeta(y_{1},t)=\zeta_{0}(y_{1})+\eta(y_{1},t)
.

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\phi(z)=0
for
z\leq\frac{1}{4}\min\zeta_{0}
and
\phi(z)=z
for
z\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\zeta_{0}
.
Now we define the mapping
\Phi:\Omega\mapsto\Omega(t)
by

(1.4)

\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},t):=\left(x_{1},x_{2}+\frac{\phi(x_{2})}{\zeta_{0}(x_{1})}\bar{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2},t)\right)=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\Omega(t),

with
\bar{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2},t):=\mathcal{P}E\eta(x_{1},x_{2}-\zeta_{0}(x_{1}),t)
,
where
E:H^{s}(-\ell,\ell)\mapsto H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
is a bounded extension operator for all
0\leq s\leq 3
and
\mathcal{P}
is the lower Poisson extension given by

\mathcal{P}f(x_{1},x_{2}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi|\xi|x_{2}}e^{2\pi ix_{1}\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

If
\eta
is sufficiently small (in proper Sobolev spaces), the mapping
\Phi
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism of
\Omega
onto
\Omega(t)
that maps the components of
\partial\Omega
to the corresponding components of
\partial\Omega(t)
. The Jacobian matrix
\nabla\Phi
and the transform matrix
\mathcal{A}
of
\Phi
are

(1.5)

\nabla\Phi=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
A&J\end{array}\right),\quad\mathcal{A}=(\nabla\Phi)^{-T}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-AK\\
0&K\end{array}\right),

with entries

(1.6)

A:=W\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}-\frac{\phi}{\zeta_{0}^{2}}\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}\bar{\eta},\quad J:=1+\frac{\phi^{\prime}}{\zeta_{0}}\bar{\eta}+W\partial_{2}\bar{\eta},\quad K:=\frac{1}{J},\quad W:=\frac{\phi}{\zeta_{0}}.

We define the transform operators as follows.

(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}f)_{i}:=\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}f,\quad\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}X:=\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}X_{i},\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}f:=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}f

for appropriate
f
and
X
. We write the stress tensor

S_{\mathcal{A}}(P,u):=PI-\mu\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u

where
I
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix and
(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u)_{ij}:=\mathcal{A}_{ik}\partial_{k}u_{j}+\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}u_{i}
is the symmetric
\mathcal{A}
–gradient. Note that if we extend
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}
to act on symmetric tensors in the natural way, then
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(P,u)=-\mu\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}u+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}P
for vectors fields satisfying
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0
.
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism justified by
[
23
]
.
We rewrite the solutions to (
1.1
) as a perturbation around the equilibrium state
(0,P_{0},\zeta_{0})
. By defining new perturbed unknowns
(u,p,\eta)
via
u=0+u
,
P=P_{0}+p
, and
\zeta=\zeta_{0}+\eta
, we can use the analysis of
[
10
]
to transform the problem (
1.1
) to the equilibrium domain
\Omega
for
t\geq 0
. For our purpose, we introduce the following system

(1.7)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u-\partial_{t}\bar{\eta}WK\partial_{2}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}u+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)=0\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega(t),\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega(t),\\
&S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\mathcal{N}=g\eta\mathcal{N}-\sigma\partial_{1}\left(\tfrac{\partial_{1}\eta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}\right)\mathcal{N}-\sigma\partial_{1}\Big(\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\Big)\mathcal{N}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma(t),\\
&(S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\nu-\beta u)\cdot\tau=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s}(t),\\
&u\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s}(t),\\
&\partial_{t}\eta=u\cdot\mathcal{N}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma(t),\\
&\kappa\partial_{t}\eta(\pm\ell,t)+\kappa\hat{\mathscr{W}}(\partial_{t}\eta(\pm\ell,t))=\mp\sigma\left(\tfrac{\partial_{1}\eta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\right)(\pm\ell,t),\end{aligned}\right.

where
\kappa:=\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(0)>0
,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{N}:=-\partial_{1}\zeta e_{1}+e_{2}
are still determined in terms of
\zeta=\zeta_{0}+\eta
.
\mathcal{R}(y,z)
is a multi-variable function defined as follows:

\mathcal{R}(y,z)=\int_{0}^{z}3\frac{(s-z)(s+y)}{(1+|y+s|^{2})^{\frac{5}{2}}}ds.

In the following, let
\mathcal{N}_{0}
be the non-unit normal vector for the equilibrium surface
\Sigma
, and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{0}-\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}
.
Since all the differential operators in (
1.7
) are in terms of
\eta
, (
1.7
) is connected to the geometry of the free surface. This geometric structure is essential to control higher-order derivatives.

1.2.
Main Theorem

We use the same notation for the equilibrium contact angle
\omega_{eq}
formed between the surface
\zeta_{0}
and the wall
\Sigma_{s}
, and the parameter
\varepsilon_{max}:=\min\{1,-1+\pi/\omega_{eq}\}
as in
[
10
]
, allowing us to choose three parameters
\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{-}
and
\varepsilon_{+}
that satisfy

(1.8)

0<\alpha<\varepsilon_{-}<\varepsilon_{+}<\varepsilon_{max},\quad\alpha<\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon_{-}}{2},\frac{\varepsilon_{+}-\varepsilon_{-}}{2}\right\},\quad\varepsilon_{+}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{-}+1}{2},

(1.9)

1<q_{-}:=\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon_{-}}<q_{+}:=\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon_{+}}<q_{max}:=\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon_{max}}<2.

Define the energy

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)

\displaystyle:=\|u\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\sum_{i=0}^{1}\|\partial_{t}^{i}u\|_{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}^{2}+\sum_{i=0}^{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}^{i}u\|_{0}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{i}p\|_{W^{1,q_{+}}}^{2}\Big)+\sum_{i=0}^{1}\|\partial_{t}^{i}p\|_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\sum_{i=0}^{1}\|\partial_{t}^{i}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta\|_{1}^{2},

and the dissipation

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)

\displaystyle:=\Big(\|u\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|p\|_{W^{1,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}\Big)+\|\partial_{t}u\|^{2}_{W^{2,q_{-}}}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}^{j}u\|_{1}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{j}u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{t}p\|_{W^{1,q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta\|_{H^{3/2-\alpha}}^{2}+[\partial_{t}^{j+1}\eta]_{\ell}^{2}\Big)+\|\partial_{t}^{3}\eta\|_{1/2-\alpha}^{2}.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that the contact angle
\omega_{eq}\in(0,\pi)
and
\varepsilon_{max}\in(0,1)
.
Suppose that the initial data
\Big(u_{0},p_{0},\eta_{0},\partial_{t}u(0),\partial_{t}p(0),\partial_{t}\eta(0),\partial_{t}^{2}u(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0)\Big)
satisfy the compatibility conditions for
j=0,1,2,

(1.12)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}D_{t}^{j}u(0)=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&D_{t}^{j}u(0)\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&D_{t}^{j}u(0)\cdot\mathcal{N}(0)=\partial_{t}^{j+1}\eta(0)\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\end{aligned}\right.

and the zero-average condition for
k=0,1,2

(1.13)

\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{t}^{k}\eta(0)=0.

Then there exists a sufficiently small
\delta_{0}>0
, such that if
\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(0)}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0)\|_{W^{2-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}\leq\delta_{0}
, then there exists a unique solution
(u,p,\eta)
to (
1.7
) for
t\in[0,\infty)
that achieves the initial data and satisfies

(1.14)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 0}e^{\lambda t}\mathcal{E}(t)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\mathcal{E}(0)

for some universal constants
C>0
and
\lambda>0
.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that the term
\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0)\|_{W^{2-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}
is stronger than the initial energy functional. This term is sufficient to guarantee that our initial data set is non-empty (see the discussion in Appendix
B
). Since
\Phi
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism (which follows from
J>0
as well as the fact that the derivatives of
\eta
are sufficiently small), we may change coordinates from
\Omega
to
\Omega(t)
to obtain solutions to (
1.1
).

1.3.
Methodology

It is well known that the dynamical law (
1.2
) for the contact line has been established experimentally, and that this law is a key ingredient in deriving the basic energy–dissipation structure. However, unlike in most free-boundary problems, it is far from clear, from a PDE standpoint, whether such a law can be imposed as an independent boundary condition in a manner consistent with the other standard boundary conditions. In particular, one must construct a solution satisfying all boundary conditions without overdetermining the system.

To construct such a solution, we first introduce a linear system involving prescribed functions and unknowns. We then prove that the nonlinear solution map induced by this system is a contraction on a suitably chosen complete metric space endowed with an appropriate metric. This establishes the local well-posedness of (
1.1
). Combining this local result with the
a priori
estimates established in
[
10
]
, we obtain Theorem
1.1
.

The main part of this paper is devoted to constructing solutions to the linear system. This construction is carried out in the following steps.

\bullet

Pseudo-linear construction

A natural way to construct the solution to (
1.7
) is to first consider a linear system and then take temporal derivatives to obtain a higher-order system. In particular, in order to close the energy estimate at the linear level (see Section
4
), we will not attack (
1.7
) directly, and instead begin with the first-order system and equip it with the quasi-linear structure, which reads

(1.15)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}D_{t}u+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(D_{t}u,\partial_{t}p)+\partial_{t}(Ru)+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Ru)=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}D_{t}u=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial_{t}p,D_{t}u)\mathcal{N}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Ru)\mathcal{N}=g\partial_{t}\eta\mathcal{N}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}+\partial_{1}I_{1}\mathcal{N}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\eta)\mathcal{N}+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta)^{2})\mathcal{N}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial_{t}p,D_{t}u)\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial_{t}(Ru))\nu-\beta D_{t}u)\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
D_{t}u\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}^{2}\eta=D_{t}u\cdot\mathcal{N}+(Ru)\cdot\mathcal{N}-\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\eta+u_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\eta)+I_{2}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{1}\eta\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\eta)^{2})\pm I_{1}\Big)(\pm\ell)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\kappa(\partial_{t}^{2}\eta)(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7},\end{cases}

where
R
is defined in (
2.14
), and

I_{1}=\big(\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta_{0})\big)(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta_{0}),\ I_{2}=-(u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\mathcal{N})(0)
.

\bullet

Implications of the quasi-linearity

We choose not to work directly with the original system (
1.7
), but instead (
1.15
) and further (
1.16
) below, due to a technical consideration.
In the energy estimates in second order for
(\partial_{t}^{2}v,\partial_{t}^{2}\xi)
,
the following two integrals arise and cannot be controlled:

\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\eta\,\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{3}\xi\,\mathrm{d}x,\qquad\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{1}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\eta\,\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
\eta
is a given background function and
\xi
is the unknown.
These problematic terms arise from the nonlinear capillary contribution in (
1.7
), thereby revealing a loss of regularity for the boundary unknown
\xi
.

To overcome this difficulty, we instead consider the first-order time derivative version of (
1.7
), which allows us to modify the nonlinear term in (
1.15
) so that,
when establishing the energy estimates for
(\partial_{t}^{2}v,\partial_{t}^{2}\xi)
,
the above integrals are replaced by

\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi\,\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{3}\xi\,\mathrm{d}x,\qquad\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{1}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi\,\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi\,\mathrm{d}x.

The first integral can now be controlled by integration by parts in time,
while the second can be bounded via integration by parts in space.
These bounds allow us to close the energy estimates.

\bullet

Introducing the linear problem

In order to establish the local well-posedness of (
1.15
), we now introduce
the following key linear problem (formal) via replacing
(u,p,\eta)
in (
1.15
) by given functions
(u_{g},p_{g},\eta_{g})
and unknown functions
(v,q,\xi)
. For simplicity, we continue to write
(u,p,\eta)
in place of the given functions
(u_{g},p_{g},\eta_{g})
. We have

(1.16)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}D_{t}v+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(D_{t}v,\partial_{t}q)+\partial_{t}(Rv)+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}D_{t}v=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial_{t}q,D_{t}v)\mathcal{N}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\mathcal{N}=g\partial_{t}\xi\mathcal{N}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}+\partial_{1}I_{1}\mathcal{N}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta ds\mathcal{N}+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi)\mathcal{N}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}}(\partial_{t}q,D_{t}v)\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\nu-\beta D_{t}v)\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
D_{t}v\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}^{2}\xi=D_{t}v\cdot\mathcal{N}+(Rv)\cdot\mathcal{N}-\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi+u_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi)+I_{2}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{1}\xi\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi)\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\eta\pm I_{1}\Big)(\pm\ell)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\kappa(D_{t}v\cdot\mathcal{N})(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7}.\end{cases}

Equation (
1.16
) introduces a linear map from any given functions
(u,p,\eta)
to unknown functions
(v,q,\xi)
. Here,
\mathcal{K}
is the gravity-capillary operator defined via
\mathcal{K}(\xi):=g(\xi)-\sigma\partial_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\xi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}\right)
, and
\mathcal{N}(\eta)=(-\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}-\partial_{1}\eta,1)
.

For the new capillary equation in (
1.16
), we observe that

\mathcal{K}(\partial_{t}\xi)\mathcal{N}+\partial_{1}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}\Big(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta\Big)\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{1}\xi\Big)\mathcal{N}+\partial_{1}\Big(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}\Big(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta\Big)(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi)^{2}\Big)

is not a perfect time derivative. Moreover, the system is equipped with a second-order kinematic boundary condition:

\partial_{t}^{2}\xi=D_{t}v\cdot\mathcal{N}+(Rv)\cdot\mathcal{N}-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\partial_{t}u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi+u_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}^{2}\xi\Big)+I_{2},

which is likewise not a perfect time derivative.

However, when
\xi=\eta
, both expressions become total time derivatives and can therefore be used to recover the corresponding zeroth-order equation. These two observations highlight a structural subtlety: introducing a closed system directly for
D_{t}v
is conceptually unnatural without prior knowledge of
v
. While the original variables
(v,q,\xi)
can still be recovered via (1.23) below, this formulation turns the reconstruction into an indirect process. Specifically, it requires first solving for the first-order derivatives
(\partial_{t}v,\partial_{t}q,\partial_{t}\xi)
, rather than determining the variables directly from a zeroth-order PDE.

For notational clarity and simplicity, we use
(v_{d},q_{d},\xi_{d})
as a substitute for
(D_{t}v,\partial_{t}q,\partial_{t}\xi)
in (
1.16
) and rewrite the system accordingly:

(1.17)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{d}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(v_{d},q_{d})+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)+\partial_{t}(Rv)=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}}(q_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\mathcal{N}=g\xi_{d}\mathcal{N}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}+\partial_{1}I_{1}\mathcal{N}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta)\mathcal{N}+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})\mathcal{N}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}}(q_{d},v_{d})\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\nu-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
v_{d}\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}+(Rv)\cdot\mathcal{N}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi+u_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I_{2}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\eta)\pm I_{1}\Big)(\pm\ell)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\kappa(v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N})(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7},\end{cases}

where
v
and
\xi
can be recovered via solving the ODEs

(1.18)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}v=v_{d}+Rv,\quad\partial_{t}\xi=\xi_{d},\end{aligned}

with initial data given in Appendix
B
and
D
.

We now write down the weak formulation of the system (
1.17
)

Definition 1.1
.

Suppose that
\eta
is given as well as
\mathcal{A}
,
J
,
\mathcal{N}
, etc. that are determined in terms of
\eta
as in (
1.5
), (
1.6
) and
\mathcal{N}=(-\partial_{1}(\zeta_{0}+\eta),1)
. We say that a pair
(v_{d},\xi_{d})\in\left(L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{0})\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1})\right)\times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(-\ell,\ell))
is a pressureless weak solution to (
1.17
), if
(\partial_{t}v_{d},v_{d})\in(L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{W}^{*}))\times(L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{W}))
, and that
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
satisfies the weak formulation

(1.19)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v_{d},w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((v_{d},w))+(\xi_{d},w\cdot\mathcal{N})_{1,\Sigma_{0}}+(\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\xi_{d},\partial_{1}(w\cdot\mathcal{N}))_{L^{2}}+[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N},w\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}F^{1}\cdot wJ-\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}F^{4}\cdot w-\int_{\Sigma_{s}}F^{5}(w\cdot\tau)J-[F^{7},w\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}+(\partial_{t}(Rv),w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((Rv,w))

for each
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(t)
and for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, provided that
F^{1}-F^{4}-F^{5}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}
,
[F^{7}]_{\ell}\in L^{2}_{t}
,
F^{1}\in L^{2}L^{q_{-}}
,
F^{4}\in L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}
, and
F^{5}\in L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}
for some
q_{-}\in(1,2)
. All notations here can be found in Section
2
. And
(\cdot,\cdot)_{1,\Sigma_{0}}
inner product is defined to be:

(1.20)

\displaystyle(f_{1},f_{2})_{1,\Sigma_{0}}=

\displaystyle g\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}f_{1}f_{2}\mathrm{d}x+\sigma\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\frac{\partial_{1}f_{1}\partial_{1}f_{2}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathrm{d}x.

Moreover, equation(
1.19
) should be coupled with the kinematic relation

(1.21)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}+(Rv)\cdot\mathcal{N}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi+u_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I_{2}.

We note Definition
1.1
is motivated naturally from the energy identity, which is symmetric in
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
. This key symmetric nature naturally produces a self-adjoint functional structure, which enables us to invoke a Galerkin formulation in order to construct pressureless weak solutions as well as a pressure, with average zero preserving expected a priori estimates. Thanks to the coupling of the (
1.21
), with enough regularity, such a pressureless weak solution will satisfy all the boundary conditions (including the dynamical law for the contact line (
1.2
)),
except
for the stress-free boundary condition
S_{\mathcal{A}}(q_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}
. In fact, the stress-free boundary condition is satisfied up to a constant, which leads to a complete solution to the original problem with a new modified pressure in Theorem
3.5
.

\bullet

Galerkin method and the smooth given data

We employ the Galerkin method to construct a solution to (
1.17
). However, in doing so, it becomes necessary to estimate the following inner product:

\bigl(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}^{m},\partial_{1}u_{1}\,\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}^{m}\bigr)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

where
\xi_{d}^{m}
denotes the discrete surface function.

At the Galerkin level, without enhanced
H^{\frac{3}{2}-}
regularity for
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}^{m}
, this term can only be bounded as follows:

\bigl(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}^{m},\partial_{1}u_{1}\,\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}^{m}\bigr)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\xi_{d}^{m}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\,\|u\|_{H^{2+}}.

However, the
H^{2+}
-norm of
u
cannot be controlled by the available energy or dissipation functionals. Consequently, this estimate is insufficient to close the energy inequality at the Galerkin approximation level.

To overcome this difficulty, we introduce the following modified system
with smooth given functions
(u^{k},\eta^{k},\eta^{n})
:

(1.22)

{}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{d}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(v_{d},q_{d})+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(R^{n}v)+\partial_{t}(R^{n}v)=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}v_{d}=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v)\mathcal{N}^{n}=g\xi_{d}\mathcal{N}^{n}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\partial_{1}I_{1}^{n,k}\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta))\mathcal{N}^{n}+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v)\nu-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
v_{d}\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+(R^{n}v)\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}^{k}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi+u_{1}^{k}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I_{2}^{n,k}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta^{k})\pm I_{1}^{n,k}\Big)(\pm\ell)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\kappa(v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7},\end{cases}

where the
(\eta^{k},u^{k},\eta^{n})
is defined in Appendix
C
by replacing
\varepsilon
with
\frac{1}{k}
and
\frac{1}{n}
, respectively. We have the following convergence properties by (
C.2
)

(1.23)

\displaystyle\eta^{k}\rightarrow\eta~~\operatorname{in}~~L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}},\quad\partial_{t}\eta^{k}\rightarrow\partial_{t}\eta~~\operatorname{in}~~L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}~~\operatorname{as}~~k\rightarrow+\infty,

(1.24)

\displaystyle u^{k}\rightarrow u~~\operatorname{in}~~L_{t}^{\infty}W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma),\quad\partial_{t}u^{k}\rightarrow\partial_{t}u~~\operatorname{in}~~L_{t}^{2}W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma)~~\operatorname{as}~~k\rightarrow+\infty,

and

(1.25)

\displaystyle{}\eta^{n}\rightarrow\eta~~\operatorname{in}~~L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}},\quad\partial_{t}\eta^{n}\rightarrow\partial_{t}\eta~~\operatorname{in}~~L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}~~\operatorname{as}~~n\rightarrow+\infty.

Moreover,
\mathcal{N}^{n}=(-\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}-\partial_{1}\eta^{n},1)
.
\mathcal{A}^{n}
and
R^{n}
are defined by substituting
\eta
with
\eta^{n}
.
I_{1}^{n,k}
and
I_{2}^{n,k}
are defined by

(1.26)

\displaystyle{}\begin{aligned} I_{1}^{n,k}=(\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k}(0)))\partial_{1}\xi_{d0}^{n,k},\quad I_{2}^{n,k}=u^{k}(0)\partial_{1}\xi_{d0}^{n,k}.\end{aligned}

The construction of these smooth sequences is detailed in Appendix
C
, and the full definition of the initial data of
\eta^{k}(0)
,
\xi_{d}^{n,k}(0)
,
u^{k}(0)
in (
1.26
) for any
n,k
will be given in Appendix
D
.

These two sequences allow us to first show the well-posedness of the linear problem via assumed higher regularity. Then with the solution in hand, we may introduce the enhanced estimates of
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}
to further relax the regularity requirements and close the proof. Finally, passing to the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
and
n\rightarrow\infty
leads to the desired bounds.

In detail, here we introduce two different smooth approximating sequences. The first smooth sequence (with index
k
) is used to close the energy estimate at the Galerkin level. The second sequence (with index
n
) is used to enhance the regularity for geometric parameters
R
and
J
such that the divergence-free function
D_{t}^{2}v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
.

\bullet

Contraction map and Galerkin method

To complete the Galerkin estimates, in addition to introducing smooth prescribed functions, we must also modify the unknowns
\xi
and
v
in (
1.22
).

In (
1.22
), the kinematic boundary condition is

\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+(R^{n}v)\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}-\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\partial_{t}u_{1}^{k}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi+u_{1}^{k}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d}\Bigr)+I_{2}.

The second term on the right hand side of the equation above provides us the following inner product while conducting energy estimate

\bigl(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d},\partial_{1}(R^{n}v\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})\bigr)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

Without elliptic regularity for
v
, this inner product cannot be controlled at the Galerkin level, since only
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
is available. To overcome this difficulty, we replace
(v,\xi)
in (
1.22
) by prescribed functions
(v_{l},\xi_{l})
. We then combine the Galerkin scheme with a contraction mapping argument to prove that the prescribed functions coincide with the solution, namely
(v_{l},\xi_{l})=(v,\xi)
.

Accordingly, we reformulate the equation (
1.22
) to the following system:

(1.27)

{}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{d}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(v_{d},q_{d})+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(R^{n}v_{l})+\partial_{t}(R^{n}v_{l})=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}v_{d}=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v_{l})\mathcal{N}^{n}=g\xi_{d}\mathcal{N}^{n}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\partial_{1}I^{n,k}_{1}\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{l})\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta^{k})\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v_{l})\nu-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
v_{d}\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+(R^{n}v_{l})\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}^{k}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{l}+u_{1}^{k}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I^{n,k}_{2}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{l}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta^{k})\pm I^{n,k}_{1}\Big)(\pm\ell)=\kappa(v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7},\end{cases}

where
v_{l},\xi_{l}
are given functions such that

v_{l}\in L_{t}^{\infty}W^{2,q_{-}}~~~~\operatorname{and}~~~\partial_{t}v_{l}\in L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}},\quad\xi_{l}\in L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}~~~~\operatorname{and}~~~\partial_{t}\xi_{l}\in L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}},

and

v_{l}(0)=v_{0}^{n}~~~~\operatorname{and}~~~~~D_{t}v_{l}(0)=D_{t}v_{0},\quad\xi_{l}(0)=\xi_{0}^{n}~~~~\operatorname{and}~~~~~\partial_{t}\xi_{l}(0)=\partial_{t}\xi_{0}^{n}

for any prescribed
n
. The definition of initial data can be found in Appendix
D
.

Again, to close the estimate at the Galerkin step, in a similar flavor to (
1.23
) and (
1.24
), we introduce smooth approximations for
(v_{l},\xi_{l})
denoted as

\{(v_{l}^{k},\xi_{l}^{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
such that

\displaystyle v_{l}^{k}

\displaystyle\to v_{l}

\displaystyle\text{in }L_{t}^{\infty}W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}v_{l}^{k}

\displaystyle\to\partial_{t}v_{l}

\displaystyle\text{in }L_{t}^{2}W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle\xi_{l}^{k}

\displaystyle\to\xi_{l}

\displaystyle\text{in }L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}},

\displaystyle\quad\partial_{t}\xi_{l}^{k}

\displaystyle\to\partial_{t}\xi_{l}

\displaystyle\text{in }L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}},

as
k\to\infty
. The definitions for
v_{l}^{k},\xi_{l}^{k}
are given in Appendix
C
by replacing
\varepsilon
by
\frac{1}{k}
.

Substituting
v_{l},\xi_{l}
in the kinematic boundary condition and capillary equation by
v_{l}^{k},\xi_{l}^{k}
, the quasi-linear system
(
1.27
) is turned into

(1.28)

{}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{d}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(v_{d},q_{d})+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(R^{n}v_{l})+\partial_{t}(R^{n}v_{l})=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}v_{d}=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v_{l})\mathcal{N}^{n}=g\xi_{d}\mathcal{N}^{n}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\partial_{1}I^{n,k}_{1}\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{l}^{k})\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta^{k})\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v_{l})\nu-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
v_{d}\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+(R^{n}v_{l}^{k})\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}^{k}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{l}^{k}+u_{1}^{k}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I^{n,k}_{2}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi^{k}_{l}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta^{k})\pm I^{n,k}_{1}\Big)(\pm\ell)=\kappa(v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7},\end{cases}

where for notational simplicity, we still use

(v_{d},q_{d},\xi_{d})
to denote the solution of (
1.28
) for any
k,n
. This will be our starting point to construct the solution.

Notice that system (
1.28
) has the following weak form

Definition 1.2
.

Suppose that
\eta^{n}
is given as well as
\mathcal{A}^{n}
,
J^{n}
,
\mathcal{N}^{n}
, etc. that are determined in terms of
\eta^{n}
as in (
1.5
), (
1.6
) and
\mathcal{N}^{n}=(-\partial_{1}(\zeta_{0}+\eta^{n}),1)
. We say that a pair
(v_{d},\xi_{d})\in\left(L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{0})\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1})\right)\times L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{1}(-\ell,\ell))
is a pressureless weak solution to (
1.28
), if
(\partial_{t}v_{d},v_{d})\in(L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{W}^{*}))\times(L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{W}))
, and that
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
satisfies the weak formulation

(1.29)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v_{d},w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((v_{d},w))+(\xi_{d},w\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})_{1,\Sigma_{0}}+(\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{1}\xi_{d},\partial_{1}(w\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}))_{L^{2}}+[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n},w\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}]_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}F^{1}\cdot wJ^{n}-\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}F^{4}\cdot w-\int_{\Sigma_{s}}F^{5}(w\cdot\tau)J^{n}-[F^{7},w\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}]_{\ell}+(\partial_{t}(R^{n}v_{l}^{k}),w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((R^{n}v_{l}^{k},w))

\displaystyle-(I^{n,k}_{1},\partial_{1}(w\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}))_{L^{2}(-\ell.\ell)}

for each
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(t)
and a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, provided that
F^{1}-F^{4}-F^{5}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}
,
[F^{7}]_{\ell}\in L^{2}_{t}
,
F^{1}\in L^{2}L^{q_{-}}
,
F^{4}\in L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}
, and
F^{5}\in L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}
for some
q_{-}\in(1,2)
. All notations here can be found in Section
2
. And
(1,\Sigma_{0})
norm is defined in (
1.20
). Also, to derive a solution, equation(
1.29
) should be coupled with kinematic relation

(1.30)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+(R^{n}v_{l}^{k})\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}^{k}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{l}^{k}+u_{1}^{k}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I^{n,k}_{2}.

\bullet

Strong solution and the construction of the pressure

Once the weak solution
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
to (
1.29
) has been derived, we construct the pressure
\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}:=q_{d}-\bar{q}_{d}
from the pressureless
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
. Furthermore,
(v_{d},\overset{\circ}{q}_{d},\xi_{d})
is a strong solution to (
1.28
) and the difference between the real
pressure
q
and
\overset{\circ}{q}

is a constant depending only on time. We next determine the constant needed for our pressuresless weak solutions to satisfy the capillary equation. Due to the structure of the problem, we may choose such a constant as the average of the final pressure, solving the original full set
(v_{d},q_{d},\xi_{d})
of equations.

\bullet

Reconstruction of
(v,q,\xi)

After deriving the regularity for
(v_{d},q_{d},\xi_{d},\partial_{t}v_{d},\partial_{t}\xi_{d})
, it remains to establish the regularity of the zero-order terms
(v,q,\xi)
. We recover
v,q,\xi
(also depending on
k,n
) by solving the following ODE systems

(1.31)

\displaystyle{}\begin{aligned} &v(t)=v(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}v(s)\mathrm{d}s=v(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\big(v_{d}(s)+R^{n}v(s)\big)\mathrm{d}s,\\
&\xi(t)=\xi(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{d}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\quad q(t)=q(0)+\int_{0}^{t}q_{d}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\end{aligned}

with the initial conditions

D_{t}v(0)=v^{n,k}_{d0},~~~~v(0)=v_{0}^{n,k},~~~~\xi_{d}(0)=\xi_{d0}^{n,k},~~~~\xi(0)=\xi_{0}^{n,k},~~~~q_{d}(0)=q_{d0}^{n,k},~~~~q(0)=q_{0}^{n,k},

where the definition of
v_{d0}^{n,k},v_{0}^{n,k},\xi^{n,k}_{d0},\xi_{0}^{n,k},q_{d0}^{n,k},q_{0}^{n,k}
can all be found in Appendix
D
.

After integrating in time and using the standard estimate

\|f\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}}\lesssim\|f(0)\|_{\mathcal{B}}+\int_{0}^{t}\|\partial_{t}f\|_{\mathcal{B}}ds\lesssim\|f(0)\|_{\mathcal{B}}+t^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{t}f\|_{L_{t}^{2}\mathcal{B}},

where
\mathcal{B}
can represent the norm of an arbitrary Sobolev space, we obtain the desired regularity for
(v,q,\xi)
and
(\partial_{t}v,\partial_{t}q,\partial_{t}\xi)
directly from the regularity of
(v_{d},q_{d},\xi_{d})
after integration. Moreover, this procedure preserves the boundedness of the corresponding norms.

\bullet

Passing to the limit
k,n\to\infty
and identifying a contractive mapping

After establishing the existence of a strong solution to (
1.28
), our next step is to derive bounds uniform in
k
, for fixed
n
and prescribed functions
(u,\eta,p,\eta^{n},v_{l},\xi_{l})
.

With the uniform bounds in hand, letting
k\to+\infty
, the solution to (
1.28
) converges to the solution to the following limiting system:

(1.32)

{}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}v_{d}+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(v_{d},q_{d})+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(R^{n}v_{l})+\partial_{t}(R^{n}v_{l})=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}v_{d}=0~~~&\operatorname{in}~~\Omega,\\
S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v_{l})\mathcal{N}^{n}=g\xi_{d}\mathcal{N}^{n}-\sigma\partial_{1}(\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}})\mathcal{N}^{n}+\partial_{1}I^{n}_{1}\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{l})\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta)\mathcal{N}^{n}+\sigma\partial_{1}(\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})\mathcal{N}^{n}\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
(S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(q_{d},v_{d})\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(R^{n}v_{l})\nu-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
v_{d}\cdot\nu=0~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma_{s},\\
\partial_{t}\xi_{d}=v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+(R^{n}v_{l})\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+\int_{0}^{t}(\partial_{t}u_{1}\cdot\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{l}+u_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi_{d})+I^{n}_{2}~~~&\operatorname{on}~~\Sigma,\\
\sigma\Big(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\xi_{d}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}\pm\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{zz}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})(\partial_{t}\partial_{1}\xi_{l}\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta^{k})\pm I^{n}_{1}\Big)(\pm\ell)\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\kappa(v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})(\pm\ell)-{F}^{7}.\end{cases}

Notice that for fixed
n
and
(u,p,\eta,\eta^{n},v_{l},\xi_{l})
, system (
1.32
) defines a linear mapping from
(v_{l},\xi_{l})
to
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
. After reconstructing
(v,\xi)
from
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
, we prove in Section
4
that this mapping is a contraction from
(v_{l},\xi_{l})
to
(v,\xi)
. Consequently, there exists a solution to the following system:

(1.33)

{}\begin{cases}\partial_{t}^{2}v+\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(\partial_{t}v,\partial_{t}q)=F^{1}(u,p,\eta)&\text{in }\Omega,\\[3.99994pt]
\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}D_{t}v=0&\text{in }\Omega,\\[3.99994pt]
S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(\partial_{t}q,\partial_{t}v)\mathcal{N}^{n}=g\partial_{t}\xi\,\mathcal{N}^{n}-\sigma\partial_{1}\!\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}\right)\mathcal{N}^{n}\\[1.99997pt]
\qquad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\sigma\partial_{1}\!\bigl(\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\,\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi\bigr)\mathcal{N}^{n}+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)&\text{on }\Sigma,\\[3.99994pt]
\bigl(S_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}(\partial_{t}q,\partial_{t}v)\nu-\beta\,\partial_{t}v\bigr)\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)&\text{on }\Sigma_{s},\\[3.99994pt]
\partial_{t}v\cdot\nu=0&\text{on }\Sigma_{s},\\[3.99994pt]
\partial_{t}^{2}\xi=\partial_{t}v\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n}+\partial_{t}u_{1}\partial_{1}\xi&\text{on }\Sigma,\\[3.99994pt]
\sigma\!\left(\mp\frac{\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}\pm\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\xi\right)(\pm\ell)=\kappa(\partial_{t}v\cdot\mathcal{N}^{n})(\pm\ell)-F^{7}.\end{cases}

Finally, with the help of uniform bounds in
n
, we show that as
n\to+\infty
, the solution to (
1.33
) converges to the solution to (
1.17
). This completes the construction and yields a solution to the original linear problem (
1.17
).

Also, the regularity of the limiting solution can be deduced from the uniform estimates for (
1.28
) and the convergence argument to further (
1.32
), (
1.33
) and (
1.17
).

\bullet

Construction of initial data

All the pseudo-linear and full nonlinear systems are initial–boundary value problems, and thus they rely on suitably prescribed initial data. However, from the
a priori estimates
in
[
10
]
, the energy does not propagate in time, preventing us from constructing the initial data in the usual way.

Usually, given
(u_{0},\eta_{0})
, by the Navier-Stokes system, we construct
(\partial_{t}u(0),p_{0},\partial_{t}\eta(0))
, and subsequently obtain
(\partial_{t}^{2}u(0),\partial_{t}p(0)
,
\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0))
by differentiating the Navier-Stokes system in time.

However, here we need a different yet more sophisticated approach. In Appendix
B
–
D
, we apply the backward in time derivative method (for instance, given
(\partial_{t}^{2}u(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\xi(0))
, then construct
(\partial_{t}u(0),\partial_{t}p(0),\partial_{t}\xi(0),u_{0},p_{0},\xi_{0})
). In particular,
\partial_{t}\eta(0)\in W^{2-1/q_{+},q_{+}}((-\ell,\ell))
appears naturally as part of boundary conditions. Hence, although this quantity is not included in the energy itself, it is necessary to prove that the admissible set of initial data with finite initial energy is nonempty. This constitutes the final step needed to formulate the nonlinear problem within the functional framework used in this paper.

2.
Functional Settings and Basic Estimates

In this section, we introduce the functional framework needed for the implementation of the Galerkin scheme. For simplicity, all definitions are formulated with respect to the surface function

\eta
. The corresponding functional spaces associated with the smooth surface function

\eta^{n}

are defined analogously by replacing
\eta
with
\eta^{n}
.

2.1.
Constants and Sobolev Norms

Let
C>0
denote a universal constant that only depends on the parameters of the problem,
N
and
\Omega
, but is independent of the data. This constant is allowed to change from line to line. We will write
C=C(z)
to indicate that the constant
C
depends on
z
. We write
a\lesssim b
if
a\leq Cb
for a universal constant
C>0
.

We will write
H^{k}
for
H^{k}(\Omega)
for
k\geq 0
, and
H^{s}(\Sigma)
with
s\in\mathbb{R}
for the usual Sobolev spaces. Typically, we will write
H^{0}=L^{2}
, with the exception that we will use
L^{2}([0,T];H^{k})
(or
L^{2}([0,T];H^{s}(\Sigma))
) to denote the space of temporal square–integrable functions with values in
H^{k}
(or
H^{s}(\Sigma)
).

Sometimes we will write
\|\cdot\|_{k}
instead of
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}
or
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}(\Sigma)}
. We assume that functions belong to their respective natural spaces. For example, the functions
u
,
p
and
\bar{\eta}
live on
\Omega
, while
\eta
lives on
\Sigma
. So we may write
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}}
for the norms of
u
,
p
and
\bar{\eta}
in
\Omega
, and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
for norms of
\eta
on
\Sigma
.

2.2.
Time-Independent Inner Products and Function Spaces

We define the time-independent spaces

{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega):=\Big\{u\in L^{2}(\Omega)\Big|\|u\|_{{}_{0}H^{1}}<\infty,u\cdot\nu=0\ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{s}\Big\},

endowed with the norm

\|u\|_{{}_{0}H^{1}}^{2}:=\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\beta\|u\cdot\tau\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}+[u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{0}]_{\ell}^{2}

and

\mathring{H}^{k}(U):=\left\{f\in H^{k}(U)\Big|\int_{U}f=0\right\},

where
U=\Omega
or
(-\ell,\ell)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

W:=\Big\{u\in{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)\Big|u\cdot\mathcal{N}_{0}\in\mathring{H}^{1}(-\ell,\ell)\Big\},

and

W_{\sigma}:=\Big\{u\in W\Big|\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=0\Big\}.

Throughout the paper we will often utilize the following Korn-type inequality.

Lemma 2.1
.

For any
u\in{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
and
u\in H^{0}(\Omega)
, it holds that

(2.1)

\|u\|_{1}^{2}\lesssim\|\mathbb{D}u\|_{0}^{2}+\|u\|_{0}^{2}\text{ for all }u\in H^{1}(\Omega),

Proof.

The inequality (
2.1
) may be proved in various ways. See
[
14
]
for a direct proof. It can also be derived from the Neĉas inequality
[
2
, Lemma IV.7.6 ]
.
∎

Using this lemma, we establish the following Poincaré-type inequality

Theorem 2.2
.

For any
u\in{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
, it holds that

\|u\|_{1}^{2}\lesssim\|\mathbb{D}u\|_{0}^{2}

Proof.

From Lemma
2.1
, it suffices to show that

(2.2)

\displaystyle{}\|u\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\|\mathbb{D}u\|_{0}^{2}

for any
u\in{}_{0}H^{1}
. We derive this inequality via a contradiction argument.

Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that (
2.2
) is false. Then there exists a sequence
u_{n}
such that

(2.3)

\displaystyle{}\|u_{n}\|_{L^{2}}=1,

and

(2.4)

\displaystyle{}\|\mathbb{D}u_{n}\|_{0}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}.

By Lemma
2.1
again,
u_{n}
is uniformly bounded in
H^{1}
. Therefore,
u_{n}\rightarrow u_{0}
in
H^{1}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. By equation (
2.4
), we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle{}\|\mathbb{D}u_{0}\|_{0}^{2}=0

which implies that
u_{0}=Ax+b
for some skew-symmetric matrix
A
and constant vector
b
.

Since
u_{n}\in{}_{0}H^{1}
, we have
u_{0}\cdot\nu=0
on
\Sigma_{s}
by trace theorem. Therefore, we obtain

(2.6)

\displaystyle{}(A(x,0)^{T}+b)\cdot(0,1)=0

for all
x\in(-\ell,\ell)
. Suppose that

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&a_{2}\\
-a_{2}&0\end{pmatrix},

and that
b=(b_{1},b_{2})
. We derive
a_{2}=b_{2}=0
from (
2.6
). Hence,
A=0
and
u_{0}=b=(b_{1},0)
.

Again, using the fact that
u_{0}\cdot{N}=0
on
\Sigma_{s}
, we have

u_{0}\cdot(1,0)=0

for all
y\in(0,\zeta(\ell))
. Therefore

b_{1}=0.

This implies that
u_{0}=0
, which contradicts the fact that
\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}}=0
.
∎

2.3.
Time-Dependent Inner Products and Function Spaces

Suppose that
\eta
is given, and that
\mathcal{A}
,
J
and
\mathcal{N}
, etc., are determined in terms of
\eta
. Let us define

((u,v)):=\int_{\Omega}\frac{\mu}{2}\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u:\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}vJ+\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\beta(u\cdot\tau)(v\cdot\tau)J.

We also define

(2.7)

{}(\phi,\psi)_{1,\Sigma}:=\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}g\phi\psi+\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\phi\partial_{1}\psi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}+\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\phi\partial_{1}\psi,

(2.8)

{}(\phi,\psi)_{1,\Sigma_{k}}:=\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}g\phi\psi+\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\phi\partial_{1}\psi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}+\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\partial_{1}\phi\partial_{1}\psi,

(\phi,\psi)_{1,\Sigma_{0}}:=\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}g\phi\psi+\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\phi\partial_{1}\psi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}},

(2.9)

[a,b]_{\ell}:=\kappa\Big(a(\ell)b(\ell)+a(-\ell)b(-\ell)\Big).

We denote
\|\xi\|_{1,\Sigma}:=\sqrt{(\xi,\xi)_{1,\Sigma}}
and
[\phi]_{\ell}:=\sqrt{[\phi,\phi]_{\ell}}
.

For convenience, let us define some time-dependent spaces

\mathcal{H}^{0}(t):=\Big\{u(t):\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}\Big|\sqrt{J}u\in H^{0}(\Omega)\Big\},

\mathcal{H}^{1}(t):=\Big\{u(t):\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}\Big|((u,u))+[u\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}^{2}<\infty\Big\},

{}_{0}\mathcal{H}^{1}(t):=\Big\{u(t)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(t)\Big|u\cdot\nu=0\ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{s}\Big\},

endowed with the norm
\|u\|_{{}_{0}\mathcal{H}^{1}}:=\sqrt{((u,u))+[u\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}^{2}}
, and

\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^{k}(t):=\left\{f\in\mathcal{H}^{k}(t)\Big|\int_{\Omega}f=0\right\},

where
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Finally, we define the inner products on
L^{2}([0,T];H^{k}(\Omega))
for
k=0,1
as

(u,v)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}}=\int_{0}^{T}\big(u(t),v(t)\big)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t
,
and write
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}=L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1}(t))
as the corresponding spaces with the corresponding norms
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}}
.

For some forces
\mathcal{F}^{j}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
, denote the duality

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left<\mathcal{F}^{j},\psi\right>_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T})^{\ast}}:=\int_{0}^{T}\left<\mathcal{F}^{j},\psi\right>_{(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}},\quad\left<\mathcal{F}^{j},\psi\right>_{(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}}:=\int_{\Omega}F^{1,j}\cdot\psi J-\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}F^{4,j}\cdot\psi-\int_{\Sigma_{s}}F^{5,j}(\psi\cdot\tau)J.

The following lemma implies that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(t)
is equivalent to
{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
.

Lemma 2.3
.

There exists a small universal
\delta_{0}>0
such that if
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\eta(t)\|_{W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}<\delta_{0}
,
then

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|u\|_{k}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|u\|_{k}

for
k=0,1
and for all
t\in[0,T]
. As a consequence, for
k=0,1
,

\|u\|_{L^{2}H^{k}(\Omega)}\leq\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{T}}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}H^{k}(\Omega)}
.

Proof.

For the case of
k=0
, it is directly the result in
[
8
]
. When
k=1
, we might use the similar calculation as the proof of Lemma 2.1 in
[
8
]
together with the Korn-type inequality in Lemma
2.1
and trace theory to derive the results.
∎

Now, we want to show that the time-independent spaces are related to the time-dependent spaces.
We consider the matrix

(2.11)

M:=M(t)=K\nabla\Phi=(J\mathcal{A}^{T})^{-1},

which induces a linear operator
\mathcal{M}_{t}:u\mapsto M(t)u
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Assume that
\eta\in W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}(-\ell,\ell)
. We have:

(i)
. For each
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is a bounded isomorphism from
W^{k,q_{+}}(\Omega)
to
W^{k,q_{+}}(\Omega(t))
for
k=0,1,2
; from
W^{k,q_{-}}(\Omega(0))
to
W^{k,q_{-}}(\Omega)
for
k=0,1,2
.

(ii)
. For each
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is a bounded isomorphism from
{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
to
{}_{0}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)
. Moreover,

\|Mu\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\lesssim(1+\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}})\|u\|_{1}
.

(iii)
. For each
t\in[0,T]
,
\mathcal{M}_{t}
is a bounded isomorphism from
H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}(\Omega)
to
H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}(\Omega)
.
(iv)
. Let
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. Then
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits u=p
if and only if
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(Mu)=Kp
.

(v)

M^{\top}\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{0}\ \text{on}\ \Sigma
.

Proof.

All the results are referred to
[
10
]
. The equality
J\mathcal{A}\mathcal{N}_{0}=\mathcal{N}

gives
M^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{N}=K\nabla\Phi^{\top}\cdot\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{0}
.
∎

The following proposition is also useful.

Proposition 2.5
.

If
u\cdot\nu=0
on
\Sigma_{s}
, then
Ru\cdot\nu=0
on
\Sigma_{s}
, where
R:=\partial_{t}MM^{-1}
.

Proof.

From
[
9
]
, it is known that
Mu\cdot\nu=0\Leftrightarrow u\cdot\nu=0
on
\Sigma_{s}
, which implies that

M^{-1}u\cdot\nu=0\Leftrightarrow u\cdot\nu=0
on
\Sigma_{s}
.
Then by definition of
R
,

Ru\cdot\nu=\partial_{t}MM^{-1}u\cdot\nu=-M\partial_{t}(M^{-1}u)\cdot\nu=0,

since
\partial_{t}(M^{-1}u)\cdot\nu=\partial_{t}(M^{-1}u\cdot\nu)=0
.
∎

Define the time-dependent spaces:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(t):=\{u\in{}_{0}\mathcal{H}^{1}(t):\ u\cdot\mathcal{N}\in\mathring{\mathcal{H}}^{1}(-\ell,\ell)\}

endowed with the norm
\|u\|_{\mathcal{W}}=\|u\|_{{}_{0}\mathcal{H}^{1}}+\|u\cdot\mathcal{N}\|_{H^{1}}
and the subspace
\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(t)

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(t):=\{u\in\mathcal{W}(t):\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0\}.

Define the operator
D_{t}
as

(2.14)

D_{t}u:=\partial_{t}u-Ru\quad\text{for}\quad R:=\partial_{t}MM^{-1},

with
M=K\nabla\Phi
, where
K
and
\Phi
are defined as in (
1.6
) and (
1.4
), respectively. It is easy to see that
D_{t}
preserves the
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}
–free condition, since

(2.15)

J\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(D_{t}v)=J\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(M\partial_{t}(M^{-1}v))=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(\partial_{t}(M^{-1}v))=\partial_{t}\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(M^{-1}v)=\partial_{t}(J\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}v),

where in the second and last equalities, we used the fact that
J\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}v=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(M^{-1}v)
, which is proved, according to
[
23
, Lemma A]
and the definition (
2.11
) of
M
, as

(2.16)

J\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}v=J\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}v_{i}=\partial_{j}(J\mathcal{A}_{ij}v_{i})=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(J\mathcal{A}^{\top}v)=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits(M^{-1}v).

3.
Preliminaries for the Linear System

In this section, we will introduce several preliminary results regarding the linear system (
1.17
) under the assumption that the forcing terms
F^{i}
are given and independent of the unknowns. In particular, we will show that the weak solution
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
can be used to recover the pressure
q_{d}
and further be upgraded to the strong solution under proper regularity assumptions.

3.1.
Uniqueness of Weak Solution

The definition of a weak solution was given in Definition
1.1
.
In Section
4.2
, we will see that weak solutions to (
1.19
) will arise as a byproduct of the construction of strong solutions to (
1.19
). Hence, we temporarily assume the existence of weak solutions and focus first on establishing uniqueness.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
is a pressureless weak solution to (
1.19
). Then for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}(\|v_{d}\|_{0}^{2}+\|\xi_{d}\|_{1}^{2})+\|v_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{1}}^{2}+\|v_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{0}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}+\|[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T])}^{2}\lesssim\exp(C_{0}T)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{W^{2,q_{+}}(\Omega)}+\|D_{t}u(0)\|^{2}_{H^{1+\frac{\varepsilon_{-}}{2}}}+\|\xi_{0}\|^{2}_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}+\|\partial_{t}\xi(0)\|^{2}_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}

\displaystyle+\|(F^{1}-F^{4}-F^{5})(0)\|^{2}_{(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}}+\big(1+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}\big)\big(\|F^{1}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{4}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|F^{5}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}\big)+\big(1+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}\big)\|\partial_{t}(F^{1}-F^{4}-F^{5})\|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T})^{\ast}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\|[\partial_{t}^{j}F^{7}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

where
C_{0}(\eta):=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|u\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\eta\|_{W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}
.

Proof.

The bound (
3.1
) can be directly derived by choosing
v_{d}
as the test function of equation (
1.19
). The detailed computation is given in
[
10
]

∎

Proposition 3.2
.

The weak solution to (
1.19
) is unique.

Proof.

If
(v_{d}^{1},\xi_{d}^{1})
and
(v_{d}^{2},\xi_{d}^{2})
are both weak solutions to (
1.19
), then
(v=v^{1}_{d}-v^{2}_{d},\xi_{d}=\xi^{1}_{d}-\xi^{2}_{d})
is a weak solution to (
1.19
) with
F^{1}=F^{2}=F^{4}=F^{5}=F^{7}=0
and the initial data
v_{d}(0)=\xi_{d}(0)=u(0)=D_{t}u(0)=\partial_{t}\xi(0)=0
. The bound (
3.1
) implies that
v=0
,
\xi=0
.
∎

3.2.
Pressure Estimates

Suppose that we have established the weak solution
(v_{d},\xi_{d})\in L^{\infty}H^{0}\times L^{\infty}H^{1}
such that
(\partial_{t}v_{d},\partial_{t}\xi_{d})\in L^{\infty}H^{0}\times\left(L^{\infty}H^{1}\cap L^{2}H^{3/2-\alpha}\right)
in Theorem
4.5
. We now introduce the pressure by treating it as a Lagrange multiplier and simultaneously get higher regularity of
(v_{d},\xi_{d})
to show that the weak solution coupled with the pressure is a strong solution.

Before presenting the main theorem, we establish a lemma concerning the
\|\cdot\|_{1,\Sigma}
norm defined by (
2.7
).

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that
\|\eta^{k}\|\lesssim||\eta||_{L^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}\leq\delta\ll 1
. We have

(3.2)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta^{k})\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}\lesssim||\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)||_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}\lesssim\delta.

Proof.

Using
[
10
, Theorem A.1]
, we have

\displaystyle||\partial_{1}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)||_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}\lesssim

\displaystyle||\partial_{1}^{2}\zeta_{0}\partial_{1}\eta||_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}+||\partial_{1}^{2}\eta||_{W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle||\zeta_{0}||_{W^{3,q_{+}}}||\eta||_{W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}+||\eta||_{W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}\lesssim\delta

which finishes the proof.
∎

We then introduce the following lemma which is a simple corollary of Lemma
3.3
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose that
||\eta||_{L^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{+}},q_{+}}}\leq\delta\ll 1
for a sufficiently small
\delta
to be determined. Then the following inequality holds for any
\phi\in H^{1}(\Sigma)
.

(\phi,\phi)_{1,\Sigma}\geq C(\delta)||\phi||_{H^{1}}.

Proof.

Using the definition of
(\cdot)_{1,\Sigma}
norm, we have the following computation:

(\phi,\phi)_{1,\Sigma}=\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}g\phi^{2}+\sigma\frac{(\partial_{1}\phi)^{2}}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}+\sigma\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)(\partial_{1}\phi)^{2}.

From Lemma
3.3
, we have:

||\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)||_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim||\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)||_{W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}\lesssim||\eta||_{W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}\lesssim\delta.

Therefore, we can choose a sufficiently small
\delta
such that
||\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)||_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\frac{1}{2}\inf_{x\in(-\ell,\ell)}\frac{1}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}
. Then we use this relation in the equation (
3.2
) to show that:

(\phi,\phi)_{1,\Sigma}\geq\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}g\phi\phi+\frac{1}{2}\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\phi\partial_{1}\phi}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}dx-\frac{1}{2}\inf_{x\in(-\ell,\ell)}\frac{1}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\gtrsim||\phi||_{H^{1}}

which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose that we have the weak solution
(v_{d},\xi_{d})\in L^{\infty}H^{0}\times\left(L^{2}H^{1}\cap L^{2}H^{3/2-\alpha}\right)
to the system (
1.19
) on the finite time interval
[0,T]
,
j=0,1
. Then there exists a unique pressure
q_{d}\in L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{0}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}([0,T];W^{1,q_{-}})
, such that

(3.3)

\displaystyle\|q_{d}\|_{L^{2}H^{0}}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{0}}^{2}+\|\xi_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1}}^{2}+\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1}}^{2}+\|[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}_{t}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|[F^{7}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}_{t}}^{2}+\|F^{1}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{4}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{5}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}

and

(3.4)

\displaystyle\|q_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}v_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{0}}^{2}+\|\xi_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{1}}^{2}+\|v_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{1}}^{2}+\|[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}^{2}+

\displaystyle+\|[F^{7}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}}^{2}+\|F^{1}-F^{4}-F^{5}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}.

Moreover, after deriving
q_{d}
, we may apply the ODE (
1.31
) to recover
(v,q,\xi)
, together with the following elliptic estimate.

(3.5)

\displaystyle\|v_{d}\|_{L^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}^{2}+\|q_{d}\|_{L^{2}W^{1,q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\xi_{d}\|_{L^{2}W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}v_{d}\|_{L^{2}H^{0}}^{2}+\|\xi_{d}\|_{L^{2}H^{1}}^{2}+\|v_{d}\|_{L^{2}H^{1}}^{2}+\|\xi_{d}\|_{L^{2}H^{3/2-\alpha}}^{2}+\|[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}_{t}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|F^{1}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{4}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{5}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|[F^{7}]_{\ell}\|_{L_{t}^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

Step 1 – Construction of Pressure
\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}

We state the weak formulation of the system (
1.19
):

(3.6)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v_{d},w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((v_{d},w))+(\xi_{d},w\cdot\mathcal{N})_{1,\Sigma}+[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N},w\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}F^{1}\cdot wJ-\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}F^{4}\cdot w-\int_{\Sigma_{s}}F^{5}(w\cdot\tau)J-[F^{7},w\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}+(\partial_{t}(Rv),w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((Rv,w))

for any
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}
. Define the functional
\Lambda_{t}
so that
\Lambda_{t}(w)
is defined as the difference between the left-hand side and the right-hand side of (
3.6
) with
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(t)
. By the energy estimate established in Lemma
3.1
, we obtain that
\Lambda_{t}\in\mathcal{W}^{\ast}
and
\Lambda_{t}(w)=0
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}(t)
. By
[
9
, Theorem 4.6]
, there exists a unique
\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\in\mathring{H}^{0}(t)
such that
(\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t),\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}=\Lambda_{t}(w)
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}(t)
, which is equivalent to

(3.7)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}v_{d},w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((v_{d},w))+(\xi_{d},w\cdot\mathcal{N})_{1,\Sigma}-(\overset{\circ}{q}_{d},\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+[v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N},w\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}F^{1}\cdot wJ-\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}F^{4}\cdot w-\int_{\Sigma_{s}}F^{5}(w\cdot\tau)J-[F^{7},w\cdot\mathcal{N}]_{\ell}+(\partial_{t}(Rv),w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((Rv,w)).

Moreover, by Ne
\check{\text{c}}
as inequality (for instance, see
[
2
]
), we have

(3.8)

\|\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}\|_{0}^{2}\lesssim\|v\|_{1}^{2}+\|v_{d}\|_{1}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}v_{d}\|^{2}_{0}+\|\xi_{{}_{d}}\|_{1}^{2}+\|F^{1}-F^{4}-F^{5}\|^{2}_{(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}}+\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\|\xi_{d}(s)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+|[F^{7}]_{\ell}|^{2}.

Step 2 – Elliptic Estimates for
(v_{d},\overset{\circ}{q}_{d},\xi_{d})

For a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, the triple
(v_{d}(t),\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t),\xi_{d}(t))
forms the unique weak solution to the elliptic problem.

(3.9)

{}\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\mu\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}=-\partial_{t}v_{d}-\partial_{t}(Rv)-\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)+F^{1}\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&S_{\mathcal{A}}(\overset{\circ}{q}_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}\cdot\mathcal{T}=-\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\mathcal{N}\cdot\mathcal{T}+F^{4}(u,p,\eta)\cdot\mathcal{T}\quad&\text{on}&~\Sigma,\\
&v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}=\partial_{t}\xi_{d}-(Rv)\cdot\mathcal{N}-\partial_{t}u_{1}\partial_{1}\xi_{d}\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&(S_{\mathcal{A}}(\overset{\circ}{q}_{d},v_{d})\nu+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}(u,\eta,p)\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&v_{d}\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{T}=(1,\partial_{1}(\zeta_{0}+\eta))
is the tangential vector of free surface.

Since
F^{1}(t)\in L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}(\Omega)
,
F^{4}(t)\in L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}
,
F^{5}(t)\in L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}
, and according to the elliptic theory of
[
10
, Theorem 4.7]
, the following elliptic problem

(3.10)

{}\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\mu\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}=-\partial_{t}v_{d}+F^{1}\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&-\mu\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}\mathcal{N}\cdot\mathcal{T}=F^{4}\cdot\mathcal{T}\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&v_{d}\cdot\mathcal{N}=\partial_{t}\xi_{d}+F^{4_{+}}\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&(-\mu\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}v_{d}\nu-\beta v_{d})\cdot\tau=F^{5}\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&v_{d}\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\end{aligned}\right.

admits a unique strong solution
(v_{d},\overset{\circ}{q}_{d})
obeying

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\nabla\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}+\|\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\xi_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{1}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{a}}}^{2}+\|F^{4}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|F^{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{4_{+}}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}.

In other words, the linear map
\Phi
induced by the elliptic system

\Phi:X\ni(v_{d},\overset{\circ}{q}_{d})\rightarrow(F^{1},F^{4},F^{4_{+}},F^{5})\in Y,

has a bounded inverse (The definitions of
X
and
Y
are given in Appendix (
A.1
) and (
A.2
)).

Therefore, the system (
3.9
) can be considered as a perturbation of (
3.10
). Choosing
F^{i}
to be the same in (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) for
i=1,4,5
, and
F^{4_{+}}=0
, we denote the resulting perturbation by
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
, which has the following form:

\Phi_{\varepsilon}(v_{d},\overset{\circ}{q}_{d})=\Phi(v_{d},\overset{\circ}{q}_{d})+(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}).

where

L_{1}=\partial_{t}(Rv)-\operatorname{div}_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv),\ L_{2}=-\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\mathcal{N}\cdot\mathcal{T},\ L_{3}=-(Rv)\cdot\mathcal{N}-\partial_{t}u_{1}\partial_{1}\xi_{d},\ L_{4}=(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv)\nu)\cdot\tau.

Using Hölder’s inequality and trace theorem, we have the following estimates

\displaystyle\|L_{1}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}\lesssim T\|\partial_{t}{\eta}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}+T\|\partial_{t}^{2}{\eta}\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}}\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}}\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}},

\displaystyle\|L_{2}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim T\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}},

\displaystyle\|L_{3}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim T\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}+\|\partial_{t}u\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{1+\frac{\varepsilon_{-}}{2}}}\|\xi_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}},

\displaystyle\|L_{4}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}(\Sigma)}\lesssim T\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}\|v_{d}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}.

Using the estimates above, there is an
\varepsilon_{0}
such that
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
also has a bounded inverse when
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, given that
T||\eta||_{L_{t}^{\infty}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}+T\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{3-\frac{1}{q_{-}},q_{-}}}+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}}\leq\delta\leq\varepsilon_{0}
. Therefore, the solution to (
3.9
) also obeys the same elliptic estimate as (
3.10
):

(3.12)

\displaystyle\|v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\nabla\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}+\|\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\xi_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{1}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{4}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}.

Then using Sobolev embedding
W^{2-1/q_{a},q_{a}}(-\ell,\ell)\hookrightarrow H^{3/2-\alpha}(-\ell,\ell)
and Poincaré inequality, the elliptic estimate (
3.12
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\|v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{2,q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\nabla\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}+\|\overset{\circ}{q}_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}v_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\xi_{d}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{2}H^{\frac{3}{2}-\alpha}}^{2}+\|F^{1}\|_{L_{t}^{2}L^{q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{4}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\|F^{5}\|_{L_{t}^{2}W^{1-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}.

Step 3 – Elliptic Estimates for
\xi_{d}

The weak formulation of the stress-gravity-capillary equation

(3.13)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &g(\xi_{d})-\sigma\partial_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}(\xi_{d})}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}\right)-\sigma\partial_{1}(\mathcal{R}_{z}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\partial_{1}\xi_{d})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\left(S_{\mathcal{A}}(\overset{\circ}{q}_{d},v_{d})\mathcal{N}\right)\cdot\frac{\mathcal{N}}{|\mathcal{N}|^{2}}+F^{4}\cdot\mathcal{N}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}(Rv_{d})\mathcal{N}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{N}}{|\mathcal{N}|^{2}}\quad\text{in}\ (-\ell,\ell),\\
